</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Gregory of Nyssa</b>, also known as <b>Gregory Nyssen</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Γρηγόριος Νύσσης</span></span>; c. 335 &#8211; c. 395), was <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">Bishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Nyssa_(Cappadocia)" title="Nyssa (Cappadocia)">Nyssa</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia_(Roman_province)" title="Cappadocia (Roman province)">Cappadocia</a> from 372 to 376 and from 378 until his death in 395. He is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholicism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodoxy">Oriental Orthodoxy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a>. Gregory, his elder brother <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">Basil of Caesarea</a>, and their friend <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> are collectively known as the <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Fathers" title="Cappadocian Fathers">Cappadocian Fathers</a>.
</p><p>Gregory lacked the administrative ability of his brother Basil or the contemporary influence of Gregory of Nazianzus, but he was an erudite <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theologian</a> who made significant contributions to the <a href="/wiki/Trinity" title="Trinity">doctrine of the Trinity</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Nicene_Creed" title="Nicene Creed">Nicene Creed</a>. Gregory's philosophical writings were influenced by <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>. Since the mid-twentieth century, there has been a significant increase in interest in Gregory's works from the academic community, particularly involving <a href="/wiki/Christian_Universalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian Universalism">universal salvation</a>, which has resulted in challenges to many traditional interpretations of his theology.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Background">Background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Acts" class="mw-redirect" title="Book of Acts">Book of Acts</a> depicts that on the <a href="/wiki/Day_of_Pentecost" class="mw-redirect" title="Day of Pentecost">Day of Pentecost</a> there were visiting Jews who were "residents of ...<a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a>"<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> in attendance. In the <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_of_Peter" title="First Epistle of Peter">First Epistle of Peter</a>, written after AD 65, the author greets Christians who are "exiles scattered throughout…Cappadocia". There is no further reference to Cappadocia in the rest of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Christianity">Early Christianity</a> arose in Cappadocia relatively late, with no evidence of a Christian community before the late second century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-vd1_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vd1-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Jerusalem" title="Alexander of Jerusalem">Alexander of Jerusalem</a> was the first bishop of the province in the early to mid third century, a period in which Christians suffered persecution from the local Roman authorities.<sup id="cite_ref-vd1_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vd1-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BRILL127_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BRILL127-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The community remained very small throughout the third century: when <a href="/wiki/Gregory_Thaumaturgus" title="Gregory Thaumaturgus">Gregory Thaumaturgus</a> acceded to the bishopric in c. 250, according to his namesake, the Nyssen, there were only seventeen members of the Church in Caesarea.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cappadocian bishops were among those at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicaea" title="First Council of Nicaea">First Council of Nicaea</a>. Because of the broad distribution of the population, rural bishops [χωρεπισκοποι] were appointed to support the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Caesarea_in_Cappadocia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia">Bishop of Caesarea</a>. During the late fourth century there were around 50 of them. In Gregory's lifetime, the Christians of Cappadocia were devout, with the veneration of the <a href="/wiki/Forty_Martyrs_of_Sebaste" title="Forty Martyrs of Sebaste">Forty Martyrs of Sebaste</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_George" title="Saint George">Saint George</a> being particularly significant and represented by a considerable monastic presence. There were some adherents of heretical branches of Christianity, most notably Arians, <a href="/wiki/Encratites" title="Encratites">Encratites</a> and <a href="/wiki/Euchites" title="Euchites">Messalians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BRILL127-8_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BRILL127-8-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Early life and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gregory was born around 335, probably in or near the city of <a href="/wiki/Neocaesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Neocaesarea">Neocaesarea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pontus_(region)" title="Pontus (region)">Pontus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-103_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-103-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His family was aristocratic and Christian—according to <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a>, his mother was <a href="/wiki/Emmelia_of_Caesarea" title="Emmelia of Caesarea">Emmelia of Caesarea</a>, and his father, a rhetorician, has been identified either as <a href="/wiki/Basil_the_Elder" title="Basil the Elder">Basil the Elder</a> or as a Gregory.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-103_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-103-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Among his eight siblings were <a href="/wiki/Macrina_the_Younger" title="Macrina the Younger">St. Macrina the Younger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Naucratius" title="Naucratius">St. Naucratius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Sebaste" title="Peter of Sebaste">St. Peter of Sebaste</a> and <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">St. Basil of Caesarea</a>. The precise number of children in the family was historically contentious: the commentary on 30 May in the <i><a href="/wiki/Acta_Sanctorum" title="Acta Sanctorum">Acta Sanctorum</a></i>, for example, initially states that they were nine, before describing Peter as the tenth child. It has been established that this confusion occurred due to the death of one son in infancy, leading to ambiguities in Gregory's own writings.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's parents had suffered persecution for their faith: he writes that they "had their goods confiscated for confessing Christ."<sup id="cite_ref-Lowther_Clarke_1916_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lowther_Clarke_1916-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's paternal grandmother, <a href="/wiki/Macrina_the_Elder" title="Macrina the Elder">Macrina the Elder</a>. is also revered as a saint<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-104_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-104-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and his maternal grandfather was a martyr, as Gregory put it "killed by Imperial wrath"<sup id="cite_ref-Lowther_Clarke_1916_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lowther_Clarke_1916-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> under the persecution of the Roman <a href="/wiki/Maximinus_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Maximinus II">Emperor Maximinus II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Between the 320s to the early 340s, the family rebuilt its fortunes, with Gregory's father working in the city of Neocaesarea as an advocate and rhetorician.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory's temperament is said to have been quiet and meek, in contrast to his brother Basil who was known to be much more outspoken.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Gregory was first educated at home, by his mother Emmelia and sister Macrina. Little is known of what further education he received. Apocryphal hagiographies depict him studying at <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a>, but this is speculation probably based on the life of his brother Basil.<sup id="cite_ref-Drijvers-120_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Drijvers-120-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> It seems more likely that he continued his studies in <a href="/wiki/Kayseri" title="Kayseri">Caesarea</a>, where he read <a href="/wiki/Classical_literature" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical literature">classical literature</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophy</a> and perhaps <a href="/wiki/Medicine" title="Medicine">medicine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-105_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-105-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Gregory himself claimed that his only teachers were Basil, "Paul, John and the rest of the Apostles and prophets".<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While his brothers Basil and Naucratius lived as <a href="/wiki/Hermit" title="Hermit">hermits</a> from <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;355</span>, Gregory initially pursued a non-ecclesiastical career as a <a href="/wiki/Rhetorician" class="mw-redirect" title="Rhetorician">rhetorician</a>. He did, however, act as a <a href="/wiki/Reader_(liturgy)" title="Reader (liturgy)">lector</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-105_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-105-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He is known to have married a woman named Theosebia during this period, who is sometimes identified with <a href="/wiki/Theosebia" title="Theosebia">Theosebia the Deaconess</a>, venerated as a saint by Orthodox Christianity. This is controversial, however, and other commentators suggest that Theosebia the Deaconess was one of Gregory's sisters.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Brill-106_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-106-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Episcopate">Episcopate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Episcopate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 371, the <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Valens" title="Valens">Valens</a> split Cappadocia into two new provinces, Cappadocia Prima and Cappadocia Secunda.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> This resulted in complex changes in ecclesiastical boundaries, during which several new bishoprics were created. Gregory was elected bishop of the new <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">see</a> of <a href="/wiki/Nyssa_(Cappadocia)" title="Nyssa (Cappadocia)">Nyssa</a> in 372, presumably with the support of his brother Basil, who was <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop" title="Metropolitan bishop">metropolitan</a> of <a href="/wiki/Caesarea_Mazaca" class="mw-redirect" title="Caesarea Mazaca">Caesarea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-107_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-107-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's early policies as bishop often went against those of Basil; for instance, while his brother condemned the <a href="/wiki/Sabellianism" title="Sabellianism">Sabellianist</a> followers of <a href="/wiki/Marcellus_of_Ancyra" title="Marcellus of Ancyra">Marcellus of Ancyra</a> as <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretics</a>, Gregory may have tried to reconcile them with the church.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-107_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-107-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory faced opposition to his reign in Nyssa and, in 373, <a href="/wiki/Amphilochius_of_Iconium" title="Amphilochius of Iconium">Amphilochius</a>, bishop of <a href="/wiki/Konya" title="Konya">Iconium</a>, had to visit the city to quell discontent. In 375, Desmothenes of Pontus convened a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> at <a href="/wiki/Ancyra" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancyra">Ancyra</a> to try Gregory on charges of <a href="/wiki/Embezzlement" title="Embezzlement">embezzlement</a> of church funds and irregular ordination of bishops. He was arrested by imperial troops in the winter of the same year, but escaped to an unknown location. The synod of Nyssa, which was convened in the spring of 376, deposed him.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-108_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-108-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> However, Gregory regained his see in 378, perhaps due to an amnesty promulgated by the new emperor, <a href="/wiki/Gratian" title="Gratian">Gratian</a>. In the same year Basil died, and despite the relative unimportance of Nyssa, Gregory took over many of his brother's former responsibilities in Pontus.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-109_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-109-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg/220px-Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="170" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg/330px-Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg/440px-Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3003" data-file-height="2326" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Council_of_Constantinople_381-stavropoleos_church.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The First Council of Constantinople, as depicted in a <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">fresco</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Stavropoleos_Monastery" title="Stavropoleos Monastery">Stavropoleos Monastery</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bucharest" title="Bucharest">Bucharest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Romania" title="Romania">Romania</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>He was present at the <a href="/wiki/Synods_of_Antioch" title="Synods of Antioch">Synod</a> of <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a> in April 379, where he unsuccessfully attempted to reconcile the followers of <a href="/wiki/Meletius_of_Antioch" title="Meletius of Antioch">Meletius of Antioch</a> with those of <a href="/wiki/Paulinus,_Bishop_of_Antioch" class="mw-redirect" title="Paulinus, Bishop of Antioch">Paulinus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-110_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-110-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> After visiting the village of Annisa to see his dying sister Macrina, he returned to Nyssa in August. In 380 he travelled to <a href="/wiki/Sivas" title="Sivas">Sebaste</a>, in the province of <a href="/wiki/Armenia_Prima" class="mw-redirect" title="Armenia Prima">Armenia Prima</a>, to support a pro-Nicene candidate for the election to the bishopric. To his surprise, he himself was elected to the seat, perhaps due to the population's association of him with his brother.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-111_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-111-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> However, Gregory deeply disliked the relatively unhellenized society of Armenia, and he was confronted by an investigation into his orthodoxy by local opponents of the Nicene theology.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-111_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-111-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> After a stay of several months, a substitute was found—possibly Gregory's brother Peter, who was bishop of Sebaste from 381—and Gregory returned home to Nyssa to write books I and II of <i>Against <a href="/wiki/Eunomius_of_Cyzicus" title="Eunomius of Cyzicus">Eunomius</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-111_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-111-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory participated in the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Constantinople" title="First Council of Constantinople">First Council of Constantinople</a> (381), and perhaps gave there his famous sermon <i>In suam ordinationem</i>. He was chosen to <a href="/wiki/Eulogy" title="Eulogy">eulogise</a> at the funeral of Meletius, which occurred during the council. The council sent Gregory on a mission to Arabia, perhaps to ameliorate the situation in <a href="/wiki/Bostra" class="mw-redirect" title="Bostra">Bostra</a> where two men, Agapius and Badagius, claimed to be bishop. If this is the case, Gregory was unsuccessful, as the see was still contested in 394.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-111_27-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-111-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Brill-112_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-112-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He then travelled to <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a> where <a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Jerusalem" title="Cyril of Jerusalem">Cyril of Jerusalem</a> faced opposition from local clergy due to the fact that he had been ordained by <a href="/wiki/Acacius_of_Caesarea" title="Acacius of Caesarea">Acacius of Caesarea</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Arian" class="mw-redirect" title="Arian">Arian</a> heretic. Gregory's attempted mediation of the dispute was unsuccessful, and he himself was accused of holding unorthodox <a href="/wiki/Christology" title="Christology">views on the nature of Christ</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-112_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-112-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> His later reign in Nyssa was marked by conflict with his metropolitan, Helladius. Gregory was present at a 394 synod convened at Constantinople to discuss the continued problems in Bostra. The year of his death is unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-114_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-114-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The traditional view of Gregory is that he was an orthodox <a href="/wiki/Trinitarianism" class="mw-redirect" title="Trinitarianism">Trinitarian theologian</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Ramelli_2020_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ramelli_2020-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> who was influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Neoplatonism" title="Neoplatonism">Neoplatonism</a> of <a href="/wiki/Plotinus" title="Plotinus">Plotinus</a> and believed in <a href="/wiki/Christian_universalism" title="Christian universalism">universal salvation</a> following <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> However, as a highly original and sophisticated thinker, Gregory is difficult to classify, and many aspects of his theology are contentious among both conservative <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> theologians and Western academic scholarship.<sup id="cite_ref-Ramelli_2020_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ramelli_2020-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Coakley_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Coakley-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> This is often due to the lack of systematic structure and the presence of terminological inconsistencies in Gregory's work.<sup id="cite_ref-SDOT_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SDOT-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conception_of_the_Trinity">Conception of the Trinity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Conception of the Trinity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gregory, following Basil, defined the Trinity as "one essence [<a href="/wiki/Ousia" title="Ousia">οὐσία</a>] in three persons [<a href="/wiki/Hypostasis_(philosophy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hypostasis (philosophy)">ὑποστάσεις</a>]", the formula adopted by the Council of Constantinople in 381.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Like the other Cappadocian Fathers, he was a <a href="/wiki/Homoousian" class="mw-redirect" title="Homoousian">homoousian</a>, and <i>Against Eunomius</i> affirms the truth of the <a href="/wiki/Consubstantiality" title="Consubstantiality">consubstantiality</a> of the trinity over Eunomius' Aristotelian belief that the Father's substance is unengendered, whereas the Son's is engendered.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-750_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-750-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> According to Gregory, the differences between the three persons of the Trinity reside in their differing hypostatic origin, and the triune nature of God is revealed through divine action (despite the unity of God in His action).<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-751_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-751-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The Son is therefore defined as begotten of the Father, the Holy Spirit as proceeding from the Father, and the Father by his role as progenitor. However, this doctrine would seem to <a href="/wiki/Subordinationism" title="Subordinationism">subordinate</a> the Son to the Father, and the Holy Spirit to the Son. <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenson" title="Robert Jenson">Robert Jenson</a> suggests that Gregory implies that each member of the <a href="/wiki/Godhead_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Godhead (Christianity)">Godhead</a> has an individual priority: the Son has <a href="/wiki/Epistemology" title="Epistemology">epistemological priority</a>, the Father has <a href="/wiki/Ontology" title="Ontology">ontic priority</a> and the Spirit has <a href="/wiki/Metaphysics" title="Metaphysics">metaphysical priority</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Other commentators disagree: <a href="/wiki/Morwenna_Ludlow" title="Morwenna Ludlow">Morwenna Ludlow</a>, for instance, argues that epistemic priority resides primarily in the Spirit in Gregory's theology.<sup id="cite_ref-Ludlow_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ludlow-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Modern proponents of <a href="/wiki/Social_trinitarianism" title="Social trinitarianism">social trinitarianism</a> often claim to have been influenced by the Cappadocians' dynamic picture of the Trinity.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> However, it would be fundamentally incorrect to identify Gregory as a social Trinitarian, as his theology emphasises the unity of God's will, and he clearly believes that the <a href="/wiki/Identity_(philosophy)" title="Identity (philosophy)">identities</a> of the Trinity are the three persons, not the relations between them.<sup id="cite_ref-SDOT_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SDOT-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ludlow_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ludlow-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Infinitude_of_God">Infinitude of God</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Infinitude of God">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg/220px-St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg/330px-St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fe/St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg 2x" data-file-width="331" data-file-height="450" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Gregory_of_Nyssa.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>11th-century <a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">mosaic</a> of Gregory of Nyssa. <a href="/wiki/Saint_Sophia%27s_Cathedral,_Kyiv" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Sophia&#39;s Cathedral, Kyiv">Saint Sophia Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kyiv</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>.</div></div></div>
<p>Gregory was one of the first theologians to argue, in opposition to <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>, that God is <a href="/wiki/Infinity" title="Infinity">infinite</a>. His main argument for the infinity of God, found in <i>Against Eunomius</i>, is that God's goodness is limitless, and as God's goodness is <a href="/wiki/Essence" title="Essence">essential</a>, God is also limitless.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-424_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-424-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>An important consequence of Gregory's belief in the infinity of God is his belief that God, as limitless, is essentially incomprehensible to the limited minds of created beings. In <i>Life of Moses</i>, Gregory writes: "...every concept that comes from some comprehensible image, by an approximate understanding and by guessing at the Divine nature, constitutes an idol of God and does not proclaim God."<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's theology was thus <a href="/wiki/Apophatic_theology" title="Apophatic theology">apophatic</a>: he proposed that God should be defined in terms of what we know He is not rather than what we might speculate Him to be.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-68_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-68-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Accordingly, the Nyssen taught that due to God's infinitude, a created being can never reach an understanding of God, and thus for man in both life and the afterlife there is a <a href="/wiki/Theosis_(Eastern_Orthodox_theology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Theosis (Eastern Orthodox theology)">constant progression</a> [ἐπέκτασις] towards the unreachable knowledge of God, as the individual continually transcends all which has been reached before.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-425_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-425-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In the <i>Life of Moses</i>, Gregory speaks of three stages of this spiritual growth: initial darkness of <a href="/wiki/Ignorance" title="Ignorance">ignorance</a>, then spiritual <a href="/wiki/Light_(theology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Light (theology)">illumination</a>, and finally a darkness of the mind in mystic <a href="/wiki/Theoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Theoria">contemplation</a> of the God who cannot be comprehended.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-522_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-522-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Universalism">Universalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Universalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gregory seems to have believed in the universal salvation of all human beings. Gregory argues that when Paul says that God will be "all in all" (1 Cor. 15:28), this means that though some may need to undergo a long period of purification, eventually "no being will remain outside the number of the saved"<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> and that "no being created by God will fall outside the Kingdom of God".<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Due to the unity of human nature in Christ "all, thanks to the union with one another, will be joined in communion with the Good, in Jesus Christ Our Lord".<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> By his incarnation, death and resurrection Christ achieves "the common salvation of human nature".<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory also described God's work this way: "His [God's] end is one, and one only; it is this: when the complete whole of our race shall have been perfected from the first man to the last—some having at once in this life been cleansed from evil, others having afterwards in the necessary periods been healed by the Fire, others having in their life here been unconscious equally of good and of evil—to offer to every one of us participation in the blessings which are in Him, which, the Scripture tells us, 'eye hath not seen, nor ear heard,' nor thought ever reached."<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> That this is what Gregory believed and taught is affirmed by most scholars.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> A minority of scholars have argued that Gregory only affirmed the universal resurrection.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <i>Life of Moses</i>, Gregory writes that just as the darkness left the Egyptians after three days, perhaps redemption [<a href="/wiki/Apocatastasis" title="Apocatastasis">ἀποκατάστασις</a>] will be extended to those suffering in <a href="/wiki/Hell" title="Hell">hell</a> [<a href="/wiki/Gehenna" class="mw-redirect" title="Gehenna">γέεννα</a>].<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-57_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-57-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This salvation may not only extend to humans; following <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>, there are passages where he seems to suggest (albeit through the voice of Macrina) that even the <a href="/wiki/Demon" title="Demon">demons</a> will have a place in Christ's "world of goodness".<sup id="cite_ref-Ludlow-80_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ludlow-80-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Gregory's interpretations of 1 Corinthians 15:28 ("And when all things shall be subdued unto him&#160;...")<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> and Philippians 2:10 ("That at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in heaven, and things in earth, and things under the earth")<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> support this understanding of his theology.<sup id="cite_ref-Ludlow-80_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ludlow-80-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nevertheless, in the <i>Great Catechism</i>, Gregory suggests that while every human will be <a href="/wiki/Resurrection" title="Resurrection">resurrected</a>, salvation will only be accorded to the <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptised</a>, although he also states that others driven by their passions can be saved after being purified by fire.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> While he believes that there will be no more evil in the hereafter, it is arguable that this does not preclude a belief that God might justly damn sinners for eternity.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-59_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-59-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Thus, the main difference between Gregory's conception of ἀποκατάστασις and that of Origen would be that Gregory believes that mankind will be collectively returned to sinlessness, whereas Origen believes that personal salvation will be universal.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-59_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-59-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> This interpretation of Gregory has been criticized recently, however.<sup id="cite_ref-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._433-4_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._433-4-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, this interpretation is explicitly contradicted<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="It is not the interpretation of any episcopal authority (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> in the "Great Catechism" itself, for at the end of chapter XXXV Gregory declares that those who have not been purified by water through baptism will be purified by fire in the end, so that "their nature may be restored pure again to God". On the contrary, Saint Gregory also affirms that "without the laver of regeneration it is impossible for the man to be in the resurrection", meaning that the salvation will not be universal.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, in the next chapter (ch. XXXVI), Gregory says that those who are purified from evil will be admitted into the "heavenly company".<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Attempting to reconcile these disparate positions, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> theologian Dr. Mario Baghos notes that "when taken at face value the saint seems to be contradicting himself in these passages; on the one hand he asserted the salvation of all and the complete eradication of evil, and, on the other, that the fire needed to purge evil is 'sleepless', i.e. everlasting. The only solution to this inconsistency is to view any allusion to universal salvation in St Gregory as an expression of God's intention for humanity, which is in fact attested to when his holy sister states that God has "one goal ... some straightway even in this life purified from evil, others healed hereafter through fire for the appropriate length of time." That we can choose either to accept or ignore this purification is confirmed by the saint's many exhortations that we freely undertake the virtuous path."<sup id="cite_ref-Baghos_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Baghos-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Dr. <a href="/wiki/Ilaria_Ramelli" title="Ilaria Ramelli">Ilaria Ramelli</a> has made the observation that for Gregory free will was compatible with universal salvation, since every person would eventually accept the good having gone through purification.<sup id="cite_ref-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._433-4_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._433-4-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, some interpret Gregory as conceding that Judas and similar sinners will never be completely purified when he wrote, "that which never existed is to be preferred to that which has existed in such sin. For, as to the latter, on account of the depth of the ingrained evil, the chastisement in the way of purgation will be extended into infinity".<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> However, Ramelli renders the original Greek "εἰς ἄπειρον παρατείνεται ἡ διὰ τῆς καθάρσεως κόλασις" as "the punishment provided for the purpose of purification will tend to an indefinite duration."<sup id="cite_ref-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._411_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ilaria_Ramelli_2013_pp._411-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
Additional sources are needed to interpret correctly the questioned chapter.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Anthropology">Anthropology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Anthropology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Gregory's <a href="/wiki/Christian_anthropology" title="Christian anthropology">anthropology</a> is founded on the ontological distinction between the <a href="/wiki/Ex_nihilo" class="mw-redirect" title="Ex nihilo">created</a> and uncreated. Man is a material creation, and thus limited, but infinite in that his <a href="/wiki/Immortal_soul" class="mw-redirect" title="Immortal soul">immortal soul</a> has an indefinite capacity to grow closer to the divine.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Gregory believed that the soul is created simultaneous to the creation of the body (in opposition to Origen, who believed in <a href="/wiki/Preexistence" class="mw-redirect" title="Preexistence">preexistence</a>), and that <a href="/wiki/Embryo" title="Embryo">embryos</a> were thus persons. To Gregory, the human being is exceptional, being created in the <a href="/wiki/Image_of_God" title="Image of God">image of God</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Humanity is <a href="/wiki/Theomorphism" title="Theomorphism">theomorphic</a> both in having self-awareness and <a href="/wiki/Free_will" title="Free will">free will</a>, the latter which gives each individual existential power, because to Gregory, in disregarding God one negates one's own existence.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In the <i>Song of Songs</i>, Gregory metaphorically describes human lives as paintings created by apprentices to a master: the apprentices (the human wills) imitate their master's work (the life of Christ) with beautiful colors (<a href="/wiki/Virtues" class="mw-redirect" title="Virtues">virtues</a>), and thus man strives to be a reflection of Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-42_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-42-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Gregory, in stark contrast to most thinkers of his age, saw great beauty in <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Man" class="mw-redirect" title="Fall of Man">the Fall</a>: from Adam's sin from two perfect humans would eventually arise myriad.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-42_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-42-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Slavery">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Gregory was among the early Christian voices to write against slavery, declaring the institution inherently sinful.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-hart2001_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hart2001-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>If [man] is in the likeness of God, ... who is his buyer, tell me? Who is his seller? To God alone belongs this power; or rather, not even to God himself. [...] God would not therefore reduce the human race to slavery, since [God] himself, when we had been enslaved to sin, spontaneously recalled us to freedom. But if God does not enslave what is free, who is he that sets his own power above God's?</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>St. Gregor of Nyssa, Homilies on Ecclesiastes, The evils of slave-owning; Hall and Moriarty, trs., de Gruyter (New York, 1993) p. 74.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Gregory used Plato's definition of virtue as ‘something that admits of no master [ἀδέσποτον‎]’ in the service of his own theological arguments against slavery: (1) each human is an image of God and therefore free, (2) the equality of all humans reflects the equality of the divine Persons and (3) just as the divine nature cannot be divided into slavery (δουλεία‎) and mastery (δυναστεία‎, κυριότης‎), neither can human nature; the whole creation is a slave, but of God alone.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the stoic <a href="/wiki/Seneca_the_Younger" title="Seneca the Younger">Seneca</a> had criticized cruel slave masters and <a href="/wiki/Letter_47_(Seneca)" title="Letter 47 (Seneca)">advised slave masters to treat slaves with kindness</a> (or at least those of good character), the stoics never questioned the institution of slavery, which was considered an ordinary part of daily life in the ancient world.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Gregory of Nyssa's critique was the first and only sustained critique of the institution of slavery itself made in the ancient world.<sup id="cite_ref-hart2001_76-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hart2001-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Neoplatonism">Neoplatonism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Neoplatonism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>There are many similarities between Gregory's <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> and <a href="/wiki/Neoplatonism" title="Neoplatonism">neoplatonist</a> <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophy</a>, especially that of <a href="/wiki/Plotinus" title="Plotinus">Plotinus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Specifically, they share the idea that the reality of God is completely inaccessible to human beings and that man can only come to see God through a spiritual journey in which knowledge [<a href="/wiki/Gnosis" title="Gnosis">γνῶσις</a>] is rejected in favour of <a href="/wiki/Meditation" title="Meditation">meditation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Gregory does not refer to any neoplatonist philosophers in his work, and there is only one disputed passage which may directly quote Plotinus.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Considering this, it seems possible that Gregory was familiar with Plotinus and perhaps other figures in neoplatonism. However, some significant differences between neoplatonism and Gregory's thought exist, such as Gregory's statement that beauty and goodness are equivalent, which contrasts with Plotinus' view that they are two different qualities.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> However Plotinus does say "<i>And Beauty, this Beauty which is also the Good</i>" implying the Platonist One which is the Good is also Beauty.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> theologians are generally critical of the theory that Gregory was influenced by neoplatonism. For example, <a href="/wiki/Hierotheos_(Vlachos)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hierotheos (Vlachos)">Metropolitan Hierotheos of Nafpaktos</a> argues in <i>Life After Death</i> that Gregory opposed all philosophical (as opposed to theological) endeavour as tainted with worldliness.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> This view is supported by <i>Against Eunomius</i>, where Gregory denounces Eunomius for placing the results of his systematic <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotelean</a> philosophy above the traditional teachings of the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-750_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-750-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_day">Feast day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Feast day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> commemorate Gregory of Nyssa on 10 January. The Roman Martyrology and the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a><sup id="cite_ref-lff2018_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lff2018-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> commemorate his death on 9 March. In modern Roman Catholic calendars which include the feast of St. Gregory, such as the <a href="/wiki/Benedictines" title="Benedictines">Benedictines</a>, his feast day is observed on 10 January.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church%E2%80%93Missouri_Synod" title="Lutheran Church–Missouri Synod">Lutheran Church–Missouri Synod</a> commemorates Gregory along with the other <a href="/wiki/Cappadocian_Fathers" title="Cappadocian Fathers">Cappadocian Fathers</a> on 10 January.
</p><p>Gregory is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> (with <a href="/wiki/Macrina_the_Younger" title="Macrina the Younger">Macrina</a>) in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">lesser festival</a> on 19 July.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif/lossy-page1-220px-De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif/lossy-page1-330px-De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif/lossy-page1-440px-De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2544" data-file-height="3357" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:De_virginitate_V00110_00000008.tif" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>De virginitate</i></div></div></div>
<p>Gregory is revered as a saint. However, unlike the other Cappadocian fathers, he is not a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>. He is venerated chiefly in the East. His <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> were held by the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican</a> until 2000, when a portion of them were translated to the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox">Greek Orthodox</a> church of St. Gregory of Nyssa, <a href="/wiki/San_Diego" title="San Diego">San Diego</a>, <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Professor of theology, Natalie Carnes wrote: "One reason Gregory was not taken up into the theological stream in the West is that he was little translated into Latin. <a href="/wiki/John_Scotus_Eriugena" title="John Scotus Eriugena">John Scotus Eriugena</a> (c. 800–c. 877) should be greatly credited for the influence Gregory did have. Not only was Eriugena himself influenced by Gregory, but he also translated <i>On the Making of the Human</i> into Latin."<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Gregory's work received little scholarly attention in the West until the mid-twentieth century, and he was historically treated as a minor figure in comparison to Basil the Great or Gregory of Nazianzus.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-170_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-170-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> As late as 1942, <a href="/wiki/Hans_Urs_von_Balthasar" title="Hans Urs von Balthasar">Hans Urs von Balthasar</a> wrote that his work was virtually unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> However, the 6th-century Latin translation of <i>De opificio hominis</i> by <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_Exiguus" title="Dionysius Exiguus">Dionysius Exiguus</a> was very widespread in the Medieval period, and Francisco Bastitta Harriet argues that Nyssen's conceptions of indeterminate human nature and ontological freedom were among the core influences on Renaissance anthropology, particularly on the works of <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_of_Cusa" title="Nicholas of Cusa">Nicholas of Cusa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Pico_della_Mirandola" title="Giovanni Pico della Mirandola">Giovanni Pico della Mirandola</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> "The renewed enthusiasm of 15th-century philosophers and humanists for classical antiquity also led to a revival of the study and translation of Greek patristic works. Against this background, some of Gregory of Nyssa’s works which remained unknown to the West during the medieval centuries received their first Latin translations by leading representatives of Italian and Byzantine culture."<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> These include the early Renaissance translations of <i>De vita Moysis</i> by <a href="/wiki/George_of_Trebizond" title="George of Trebizond">George of Trebizond</a> in 1446, of <i>De vita Macrinae</i> and <i>De anima et resurrectione</i> by <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Balbi" title="Pietro Balbi">Pietro Balbi</a> between 1465 and 1473, and of <i>De oratione dominica</i> by the Byzantine scholar Athanasius Chalkeopoulos around 1465.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In part due to the scholarship of Balthasar and <a href="/wiki/Jean_Dani%C3%A9lou" title="Jean Daniélou">Jean Daniélou</a>, by the 1950s Gregory had become the subject of much serious theological research, with a critical edition of his work published (<i>Gregorii Nysseni Opera</i>), and the founding of the International Colloquium on Gregory of Nyssa.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-170_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-170-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> This attention has continued to the present day. Modern studies have focused on Gregory's <a href="/wiki/Eschatology" title="Eschatology">eschatology</a> rather than his more dogmatic writings, and he has gained a reputation as an unconventional thinker whose thought arguably prefigures <a href="/wiki/Postmodernism" title="Postmodernism">postmodernism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Major figures in contemporary research include <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Coakley" title="Sarah Coakley">Sarah Coakley</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Zizioulas" title="John Zizioulas">John Zizioulas</a> and Robert Jenson.<sup id="cite_ref-Brill-171_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-171-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Brill-172_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brill-172-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> In 2003, theologian <a href="/wiki/David_Bentley_Hart" title="David Bentley Hart">David Bentley Hart</a> published a book seemingly influenced by Gregory.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Commentary_on_Gregory">Commentary on Gregory</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Gregory_of_Nyssa&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Commentary on Gregory">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 787 AD, the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Nicea" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Council of Nicea">Second Council of Nicea</a>, the seventh <a href="/wiki/First_seven_Ecumenical_Councils" class="mw-redirect" title="First seven Ecumenical Councils">Ecumenical Council of the Church</a>, honored Gregory of Nyssa:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Let us then, consider who were the venerable doctors and indomitable champions of the Church [including] Gregory Primate of Nyssa, who all have called the father of fathers.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Henry_Fairfield_Osborn" title="Henry Fairfield Osborn">Henry Fairfield Osborn</a> wrote in his work on the <a href="/wiki/History_of_evolutionary_thought" title="History of evolutionary thought">history of evolutionary thought</a>, <a href="https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/From_the_Greeks_to_Darwin" class="extiw" title="wikiquote:From the Greeks to Darwin"><i>From the Greeks to Darwin</i></a> (1894):
</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Among the Christian Fathers the movement towards a partly naturalistic interpretation of the order of Creation was made by Gregory of Nyssa in the fourth century, and was completed by <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine</a> in the fourth and fifth centuries. ...[Gregory] taught that Creation was potential. God imparted to matter its fundamental properties and laws. The objects and completed forms of the Universe developed gradually out of chaotic material.
<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Meredith&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Anthony Meredith (page does not exist)">Anthony Meredith</a> writes of Gregory's mystical and apophatic writings in his book <i>Gregory of Nyssa (The Early Church Fathers)</i> (1999): 
</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Gregory has often been credited with the discovery of mystical theology, or rather with the perception that darkness is an appropriate symbol under which God can be discussed. There is much truth in this....Gregory seems to have been the first Christian writer to have made this important point.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</blockquote>
<p><a href="/w/index.php?title=J._Kameron_Carter&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="J. Kameron Carter (page does not exist)">J. Kameron Carter</a> writes about Gregory's stance on slavery, in the book <i>Race a Theological Account </i>(2008):
</p>
<blockquote><p>What interests me is the defining features of Gregory's vision of the just society: his unequivocal stance against 'the peculiar institution of slavery' and his call for the manumission of all slaves. I am interested in reading Gregory as a fourth century abolitionist intellectual....His outlook surpassed not only St. Paul's more moderate (but to be fair to Paul, in his moment, revolutionary) stance on the subject but also those of all ancient intellectuals -- Pagan, Jewish and Christian - from Aristotle to Cicero and from Augustine in the Christian West to his contemporary, the golden mouthed preacher himself, John Crysotom in the East. Indeed, the world would have to wait another fifteen centuries -- until the nineteenth century, late into the modern abolitionist movement -- before such an unequivocal stance against slavery would appear again.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>
Catholic theologian and author <a href="/wiki/Hans_Urs_von_Balthasar" title="Hans Urs von Balthasar">Hans Urs von Balthasar</a>, describes Gregory in his book <i>Presence and Thought: An Essay on the Religious Philosophy of Gregory of Nyssa (1988):</i><sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup></p><blockquote><p>Less prolific than Origen, less cultivated than Gregory Nazianzen, less practical than Basil, Gregory of Nyssa nonetheless outstrips them all in the profundity of his thought.</p></blockquote>

</p>

Eugene, Oregon. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781630876678" title="Special:BookSources/9781630876678"><bdi>9781630876678</bdi></a>. <a href="/wiki/OCLC_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="OCLC (identifier)">OCLC</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.worldcat.org/oclc/903899756">903899756</a>. <q>The same year Coakley's book was published, one of the contributors to that volume released his own book drawing considerably on Gregory: David Bentley Hart published The Beauty of the Infinite. Hart writes systematic theology in the tradition of Gregory, yet in conversation with contemporary thinkers. He writes Nyssen theology in the same way theologians for years have written Augustinian theology.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Beauty%3A+A+Theological+Engagement+with+Gregory+of+Nyssa&amp;rft.place=Eugene%2C+Oregon&amp;rft.date=2014&amp;rft_id=info%3Aoclcnum%2F903899756&amp;rft.isbn=9781630876678&amp;rft.aulast=Natalie&amp;rft.aufirst=Carnes&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AGregory+of+Nyssa" class="Z3988"></span></span>
